Zolpidem: (Major) Concomitant use of oxycodone with zolpidem may lead to additive respiratory and/or CNS depression. Hypotension, profound sedation, coma, respiratory depression, or death may occur. In addition, sleep-related behaviors, such as sleep-driving, are more likely to occur during concurrent use of zolpidem and other CNS depressants than with zolpidem alone. Prior to concurrent use, assess the level of tolerance to CNS depression that has developed, the duration of use, and the patient's overall response to treatment. Consider the patient's use of alcohol or illicit drugs. If zolpidem is used concurrently with oxycodone, a reduced dosage of oxycodone and/or zolpidem is recommended; use an initial dose of oxycodone at 1/3 to 1/2 the usual dosage. For Intermezzo brand of sublingual zolpidem tablets, reduce the dose to 1.75 mg/night. Monitor for sedation and respiratory depression.
Zuranolone: (Major) Avoid concomitant use of opioids and other CNS depressants, such as zuranolone. Concomitant use can increase the risk of respiratory depression, hypotension, profound sedation, and death. If alternate treatment options are inadequate and coadministration is necessary, limit dosages and durations to the minimum required, monitor patients closely for respiratory depression and sedation, and consider prescribing naloxone for the emergency treatment of opioid overdose.

Adverse Reactions
  Headline
  Adverse Reactions
  General Information
  GI adverse effects are most commonly reported with oxycodone. Opiate analgesics can cause a variety of effects, most commonly nausea/vomiting (23—27%/12—14%) and constipation (23—26%). Nausea and vomiting is more commonly seen at the initiation of opioid therapy or when switching agents. Opiate agonists affect the vestibular system and may cause more nausea/vomiting in ambulatory patients than bedridden patients. Scheduled treatment with an antiemetic during the first 1—2 days, then as needed during opiate therapy will usually control these symptoms until tolerance develops. If patients have nausea associated with movement, an antivertigo agent such as meclizine may be appropriate. Metoclopramide may be helpful in treating the symptoms of early satiety or fullness. Constipation due to decrease GI motility and secretions is common during opiate agonist therapy. Tolerance rarely develops to this effect, therefore, patients require a bowel regimen consisting of a stool softener and mild stimulant throughout opioid therapy. If the patient does not have a bowel movement for 2 days, an enema or suppository should be administered to prevent impaction. Other GI effects reported with oxycodone include anorexia, diarrhea, abdominal pain, dyspepsia, gastritis, and hiccups.Opiate agonists present the potential for abuse or psychological dependence although, the true risk of this phenomenon is extremely low in patients treated with severe pain. Data are not available to establish the true incidence of addiction in chronic pain patients. Physiological dependence, however, will occur during chronic opiate agonist therapy as evidenced by a withdrawal syndrome occurring after abrupt discontinuation of the drug in these patients. Symptoms of withdrawal include nausea and diarrhea, coughing, lacrimation, yawning, sneezing, rhinorrhea, profuse sweating, twitching muscles, abdominal and muscles pain/cramps, hot and cold flashes, and piloerection. Elevations in body temperature, respiratory rate, heart rate, and blood pressure may also occur. Without treatment most symptoms resolve within 5—14 days; however, there appears to be a secondary or chronic abstinence syndrome which may last 2—6 months, characterized by insomnia, irritabilitity and muscle aches. The shorter the onset and duration of action of the opiate agonist, the greater the intensity and rapidity of onset of withdrawal symptoms. Routine use of opiate agonists by an expectant mother can lead to depressed respiration in the newborn and a neonatal abstinence syndrome. Withdrawal symptoms in a newborn occur 1—4 days after birth and include generalized tremors, hypertonicity with any form of tactile stimuli, hyperalterness, sleeplessness, excessive crying, vomiting, diarrhea, yawning and fever. It is important to differentiate physiological dependence, the onset of a withdrawal syndrome upon abrupt discontinuation of the drug from psychological dependence. Psychological dependence is a behavioral syndrome characterized by drug craving, overwhelming concern with acquisition of the drug and other drug-related behaviors such as drug selling and seeking the drug from multiple sources.Pharmacologic tolerance to the analgesic affects of opiate agonists including oxycodone has been reported in some patients. Tolerance is the need for increasing opioid doses to maintain initial pain relief. Typically, tolerance presents as a decrease in the duration of analgesia and can be managed by increasing the opioid dose or frequency. There is no limit to tolerance, thus some patients may require very large doses of opioid analgesics to control their pain. When increasing doses of analgesia are required causes may be multi-factorial including tolerance, progression of disease or psychologic distress.The most significant adverse effect associated with opiate agonist use is respiratory depression. Respiratory depression is more common in elderly or debilitated patients, following large initial doses in non-opioid tolerant patients or when opioids are given with other agents which cause CNS depression. When oxycodone is appropriately titrated, the risk of respiratory depression is generally small as tolerance rapidly develops to this effect. Caution needs to be employed in patients with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease or decreased pulmonary reserve, cardiovascular disease, or who are taking other CNS depressants. Severe symptomatic respiratory depression should be treated cautiously with an opiate antagonist such as naloxone. Patients may also develop respiratory depression through sub-acute overdose of an opiate agonist in which sedation builds up slowly leading to a decreased respiratory rate and then respiratory failure. The risk of this is more common with methadone but may be seen with other long-acting opioids or in patients with renal or hepatic impairment. This can be managed by holding 1—2 doses of the opioid and then restarting the opioid at 25% of the previous dose and slowly increasing the dose once the symptoms have resolved.Oxycocone's effect on opiate receptors in the reticular activating system and striatum produces sedation (24%). Patients should be warned that activity requiring mental alertness can be affected because CNS depression, including drowsiness, confusion and dizziness (13%), can occur. Tolerance to the CNS depression develops within a few days; however, if it does not resolve, sedation may be managed with stimulants such as methylphenidate. Headache (6—7%), nervousness, insomnia, abnormal dreams, impaired cognition, dysphoria, euphoria, lightheadedness, alterations of mood (feelings of floating, disorientation, apprehension), anxiety, and restlessness may be seen with oxycodone therapy. Hallucinations (especially of bugs and spiders) and seizures have been reported in patients receiving high-doses of opioid agonists. Depending upon the individual patient tolerance, hallucinations may be reported in patients undergoing rapid dose escalation. Overuse of oxycodone by headache-prone patients frequently produces drug-induced rebound headache accompanied by dependence on symptomatic medication, tolerance (refractoriness to prophylactic medication), and withdrawal symptoms. In this case, overuse of oxycodone products has been defined as taking 3 or more doses per day more often than 2 days per week. The frequency of use may be more important than the dose. Features of a rebound headache include morning headache, end-of-dosing interval headache, or headache improvement with discontinuation of overused medication. Stopping the symptomatic medication may result in a period of increased headache and then headache improvement. Analgesic overuse may be responsible for the transformation of episodic migraine or episodic tension headache into daily headache and may perpetuate the syndrome.Opiate analgesics can cause spasm of the sphincter of Oddi, with morphine producing greater effects than meperidine and meperidine producing greater effects than codeine. The incidence with oxycodone is not known. Hyperamylasemia, secondary to drug-induced spasm of the sphincter of Oddi, has been associated with various opiate agonists. While serum amylase and lipase concentrations can rise as a result of biliary obstruction or spasm, overt pancreatitis is rare with opiate analgesics. Hepatotoxicity is relatively rare with these agents.Oxycodone and other opiate agonists are known to cause pruritus (13%). In the absence of a rash or other symptoms, the pruritus is probably not related to an allergic reaction. Opiate agonist-induced pruritus is thought to be mediated through histamine release through stimulation of central opiate receptors since opiate antagonists (i.e., nalmefene or naloxone) have been shown to ameliorate pruritus associated with cholestasis. Use of H1-blockers or changing to a different opioid may lessen the pruritus. Rash (unspecified) is reported in 1—5% of patients.Endocrine effects can occur during use of opiate agonists. A drug-induced decrease in thyrotropin release leads to a decrease in thyroid hormone. Morphine and related compounds can stimulate the release of vasopressin (ADH). Hyponatremia can occur as a result of SIADH. The incidence of these effects during oxycodone therapy is not known. Libido decrease can occur from chronic opiate use, following a decrease in production of luteinizing hormone and subsequently in testosterone. Women may experience amenorrhea and infertility and men may be unable to attain or maintain an erection.Morphine and related compounds such as oxycodone can cause cardiovascular adverse reactions due to their anticholinergic effects and ability to release histamine. These reactions include sinus bradycardia, palpitations, hypertension, hypotension, and syncope. Orthostatic hypotension (1—5%) may arise as a secondary effect from peripheral vasodilatation. In cases of severe respiratory and/or circulatory depression, shock and cardiac arrest may occur.Anticholinergic effects occur infrequently with oxycodone. Patients can experience xerostomia (dry mouth) (6—7%), blurred vision, or urinary retention. Oxycodone, like other opiate agonists, can cause miosis. Mydriasis can occur if, during an acute overdose for example, brain anoxia develops. Therapeutic doses can increase accommodation and sensitivity to light reflex and decrease intraocular tension in both normal and glaucomatous eyes. The incidence of these effects during oxycodone therapy is not known.Myoclonia has been reported in some patients, especially those receiving high doses of opioids; muscle twitching is reported more commonly (1—5%). Clonazepam has been used to treat myclonic jerks secondary to opioids; however, caution should be used in using benzodiazepines in combination with opioids due to additive CNS depression.Asthenia and diaphoresis (sweating) have been reported in 5—7% of patients receiving oxycodone. Sweating is a common adverse effect noted by patients receiving oxycodone as compared to placebo.
  Adverse Reactions
    Headline
    abdominal pain, anorexia, appetite stimulation, constipation, dental caries, diarrhea, dyspepsia, eructation, flatulence, gastritis, gastroesophageal reflux, gingivitis, glossitis, hiccups, ileus, nausea, stomatitis, vomiting
    General Information
    GI adverse effects are the most common adverse events reported with oxycodone. Most commonly noted GI effects in adult clinical trials with immediate-release, extended-release tablets, and extended-release oxycodone capsules, respectively, are nausea (3% or more, 23%, 10.9% to 16.6%), vomiting (3% or more, 12%, 4.1% to 6.4%), and constipation (3% or more, 23%, 5.2% to 13%); nausea with vomiting was noted in less than 3% of study patients receiving an immediate-release formulation. In pediatric trials, vomiting (21%), nausea (15%), constipation (9%), diarrhea (6%), anorexia (5%), and decreased weight (1% to 5%) were reported in those receiving extended-release oxycodone tablets. Nausea and vomiting are more commonly seen at the initiation of opioid therapy or when switching agents. Opioid agonists affect the vestibular system and may cause more nausea/vomiting in ambulatory patients than bedridden patients. Scheduled treatment with an antiemetic during the first 1 to 2 days, then as needed, during opioid therapy will usually control these symptoms. If patients have nausea associated with movement, an antivertigo agent such as meclizine may be appropriate. Metoclopramide may be helpful in treating the symptoms of early satiety or fullness. Constipation due to decreased GI motility and secretions is common during opioid agonist therapy. Tolerance rarely develops to this effect, therefore, patients require a bowel regimen consisting of a stool softener and mild stimulant throughout opioid therapy. If the patient does not have a bowel movement for 2 days, an enema or suppository should be administered to prevent impaction. Other GI effects reported with immediate-release, extended-release tablets, and extended-release oxycodone capsules, respectively, include anorexia (less than 3%, 1% to 5%, not reported), diarrhea (less than 3%, 1% to 5%, 1% to 5%), abdominal pain (less than 3%, 1% to 5%, 1% to 5%), dyspepsia (less than 3%, 1% to 5%, not reported), gastritis (not reported, 1% to 5%, not reported), gingivitis (less than 3%, not reported, not reported), glossitis (less than 3%, not reported, not reported), and hiccups (not reported, 1% to 5%, not reported); abdominal pain and gastroesophageal reflux disease were also reported in 1% to 5% of pediatric patients receiving extended-release oxycodone tablets, and gastroesophageal reflux disease was reported in 1% to 5% of adult patients receiving extended-release oxycodone capsules. Decreased appetite (1% to 5%) was observed with the extended-release capsules. Gastrointestinal adverse reactions noted in less than 1% of adult study patients include: undefined gastrointestinal disorder, eructation, flatulence, appetite stimulation, and stomatitis. Dental caries and ileus have been reported in postmarketing experience.

    Headline
    neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome, physiological dependence, psychological dependence, tolerance, withdrawal
    General Information
    Oxycodone is a controlled substance with a high potential for abuse and psychological dependence. Abuse and addiction are separate and distinct from physiological dependence and tolerance, which can develop during chronic opioid therapy. In postmarketing studies, addiction, abuse, misuse, and fatal and non-fatal opioid overdose were observed in subjects with long-term opioid use. In a prospective, 12-month observational cohort study in individuals initiating long-term use of Schedule II opioid analgesics, 1% to 6% of subjects across 2 cohorts met the criteria for addiction. Approximately 9% and 22% of subjects newly met the criteria for prescription opioid abuse and misuse, respectively. In a retrospective, observational cohort study in individuals with new long-term use of Schedule II opioid analgesics, the 5-year cumulative incidence estimates for opioid-involved overdose or opioid overdose-related death ranged from approximately 1.5% to 4% across study sites, counting only the first event during follow-up. Approximately 17% of first opioid overdoses observed over the entire study period (5 to 11 years) were fatal. Higher baseline opioid dose was the strongest and most consistent predictor of opioid-involved overdose or opioid overdose-related death. Tolerance is characterized by a reduced response to a drug after repeated administration. Physiological dependence develops as a result of repeated drug use. It manifests with a withdrawal syndrome after abrupt discontinuation or significant dose reduction, or administration of an agent with opioid antagonist activity (e.g., naloxone). Withdrawal is characterized by restlessness, lacrimation, rhinorrhea, yawning, perspiration, chills, myalgia, and mydriasis. Other symptoms include irritability, anxiety, backache, joint pain, weakness, abdominal cramps, insomnia, nausea, anorexia, vomiting, diarrhea, or increased blood pressure, respiratory rate, or heart rate. A withdrawal syndrome (with and without seizures) was reported with oxycodone in 5% or less of subjects in clinical trials. Neonatal opioid withdrawal syndrome (NOWS) may occur in neonates and infants exposed to opioids in utero. Withdrawal symptoms may include irritability, hyperactivity, abnormal sleep pattern, high-pitched cry, tremor, vomiting, diarrhea, and failure to gain weight. Onset, duration, and severity of opioid withdrawal may vary based on the specific opioid used, duration of use, timing and amount of last maternal use, and rate of elimination by the infant. Exposed newborns should be monitored closely. NOWS can be life-threatening if not recognized and treated. Treatment should be managed by clinicians experienced with neonatal withdrawal.

    Headline
    agitation, amnesia, anxiety, ataxia, confusion, depression, dizziness, drowsiness, dysgeusia, dysphonia, dysphoria, emotional lability, euphoria, hallucinations, headache, hyperesthesia, hyperkinesis, hypoesthesia, hypotonia, impaired cognition, insomnia, lethargy, migraine, paresthesias, restlessness, seizures, suicidal ideation, vertigo
    General Information
    The effect of oxycodone on opioid receptors in the reticular activating system and striatum produces drowsiness, somnolence, or sedation (23% extended-release oxycodone tablets, 3% or more immediate-release oxycodone, 8.8% or less extended-release oxycodone capsules). Warn patients that activity requiring mental alertness can be affected due to CNS depression, including drowsiness, confusion (1% to 5%, less than 3%, not reported), and dizziness (13%, 3% or more, 1.6% to 5.7%). During pediatric trials of the extended-release tablet formulation, dizziness (9%) and drowsiness (1% to 5%) also occurred. Anxiety (1% to 5% extended-release oxycodone tablets, less than 3% immediate-release oxycodone, 1% to 5% extended-release oxycodone capsules), nervousness (1% to 5%, less than 3%, not reported), insomnia (1% to 5%, 3% or more, 1% to 5%), abnormal dreams (1% to 5%, not reported, less than 1%), impaired cognition, thought abnormalities (1% to 5%, not reported, not reported), dysphoria (1% to 5%, not reported, not reported), euphoria (1% to 5%, not reported, less than 1%), lightheadedness, and alterations of mood (feelings of floating, disorientation, apprehension) have been observed in clinical trials. Lethargy, restlessness, memory impairment, and poor-quality sleep were seen in less than 1% of patients given extended-release capsules. During pediatric trials, insomnia, anxiety, depression, and agitation were reported in 1% to 5% of patients receiving the extended-release tablet. As with use of other opioids, hallucinations have been reported in less than 1% of adults receiving oxycodone extended-release tablets and seizures have been reported in postmarketing experience during use and with drug withdrawal. Depending upon individual patient tolerance, hallucinations may be reported in patients undergoing rapid dose escalation. Other psychiatric and/or neurological disorders reported in less than 3% of adults include neuralgia, agitation, hypoesthesia, and personality disorder; those reported in less than 1% of adults include ataxia or abnormal gait, amnesia, dysgeusia, hyperkinesis, hyperesthesia, hypotonia, paresthesias, speech disorder (dysphonia), stupor, vertigo, depression, depersonalization, and emotional lability. Hypoesthesia and paresthesia were more common in pediatric trials, occurring in 1% to 5% of patients. Serious psychiatric events, including aggression, emotional lability, suicidal ideation, suicidal attempt, intentional overdose, and completed suicide have been reported with postmarketing use of extended-release oxycodone tablets. Migraine occurred in less than 3% of adults with immediate-release oxycodone and 1% to 5% with extended-release capsules. Headache occurred in 7% of adults receiving extended-release oxycodone tablets, 3% or more of those receiving immediate-release oxycodone, and 6.2% to 13.9% of those receiving extended-release oxycodone capsules. Headache (14%) was also reported in pediatric patients during oxycodone extended-release tablet trials.

    Headline
    respiratory depression
    General Information
    As with other opioid agonists, the most significant adverse effect associated with oxycodone use is respiratory depression. Respiratory depression is more common in elderly or debilitated patients, following large initial doses in opioid-naive patients or when opioids are given with other agents which cause CNS depression. During pediatric trials, decreased oxygen saturation was reported in 1% to 5% of patients receiving extended-release oxycodone tablets. When oxycodone is appropriately titrated, the risk of respiratory depression is generally small as physiological acclimation rapidly develops to this effect. Caution needs to be employed in patients with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease or decreased pulmonary reserve, cardiovascular disease, or who are taking other CNS depressants. Concomitant use of a CYP3A4 inhibitor or discontinuation of a concurrently used CYP3A4 inducer may result in excessive oxycodone plasma concentrations that could produce respiratory depression. Severe symptomatic respiratory depression should be treated cautiously with an opioid antagonist such as naloxone. Patients may also develop respiratory depression through sub-acute overdose of an opioid agonist in which sedation builds up slowly leading to a decreased respiratory rate and then respiratory failure. The risk of this is more common with methadone but may be seen with other long-acting opioids or in patients with renal or hepatic impairment. This can be managed by holding 1 to 2 doses of the opioid and then restarting the opioid at 25% of the previous dose and slowly increasing the dose once the symptoms have resolved.

    Headline
    biliary obstruction, cholestasis, elevated hepatic enzymes, pancreatitis
    General Information
    Opioid analgesics can cause spasm of the sphincter of Oddi. Although the incidence of biliary tract disorders with oxycodone is not known, cautious use is recommended in patients with biliary tract disease. Postmarketing experience includes reports of cholestasis and elevated hepatic enzymes; during pediatric trials, increased alanine aminotransferase (ALT) was reported in 1% to 5% of patients. Increased gamma-glutamyl transferase was observed in less than 1% of patients during clinical trials of oxycodone extended-release capsules. Hyperamylasemia, secondary to drug-induced spasm of the sphincter of Oddi, has been associated with various opioid agonists. While serum amylase and lipase concentrations can rise as a result of biliary obstruction or spasm, overt pancreatitis is rare with opioid analgesics. Hepatotoxicity is relatively rare with these agents.

    Headline
    anaphylactic shock, anaphylactoid reactions, pruritus, rash (unspecified), urticaria
    General Information
    Oxycodone and other opioid agonists are known to cause pruritus (13% with extended-release oxycodone tablets, 2.6% to 7.4% with extended-release capsules, 3% or more with immediate-release oxycodone). Pruritus occurred in 6% of pediatric patients during extended-release tablet trials. Urticaria has been reported less frequently. In addition, allergic reactions have been noted in less than 3% of adult study patients and symptoms associated with anaphylactic shock or anaphylactoid reactions (including urticaria) have been reported in postmarketing experience. In the absence of a rash or other symptoms, the pruritus is probably not related to an allergic reaction. Opioid agonist-induced pruritus is thought to be mediated through histamine release through stimulation of central opioid receptors since opioid antagonists (i.e., nalmefene or naloxone) have been shown to ameliorate pruritus associated with cholestasis. Use of H1-blockers or changing to a different opioid may lessen the pruritus. Rash (unspecified) has been reported in 1% to 5% of patients receiving extended-release and in less than 3% of patients receiving immediate-release formulations of oxycodone in premarketing trials. Excoriation (1% to 5%) was observed with extended-release capsules during clinical trials.

    Headline
    amenorrhea, gonadal suppression, hypochloremia, hyponatremia, impotence (erectile dysfunction), infertility, libido decrease, SIADH
    General Information
    Opioid agonists can interfere with the endocrine system by inhibiting the secretion of adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH), cortisol, and luteinizing hormone (LH), and by stimulating secretion of prolactin, growth hormone (GH), insulin, and glucagon. Chronic opioid use may influence the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal axis, leading to hormonal changes that may manifest as hypogonadism (gonadal suppression). Although the exact causal role of opioids in the clinical manifestations of hypogonadism is unknown, patients could experience libido decrease, impotence, amenorrhea, or infertility. Amenorrhea has been reported in oxycodone postmarketing experience, and impotence (erectile dysfunction) was reported in less than 1% of adult study patients. Other various medical, physical, lifestyle, and psychological stressors may influence gonadal hormone concentrations; these stressors have not been adequately controlled for in clinical studies with opioids. Patients presenting with signs or symptoms of androgen deficiency should undergo laboratory evaluation. Opioid agonists can inhibit the release of thyrotropin, leading to a decrease in thyroid hormone. Oxycodone and related compounds can stimulate the release of vasopressin (ADH). Hyponatremia can occur as a result of SIADH. Hyponatremia and hypochloremia have been reported in 1% to 5% of pediatric patients during extended-release oxycodone trials; hyponatremia and SIADH have also been reported in postmarketing use.

    Headline
    adrenocortical insufficiency
    General Information
    Opioids may interfere with the endocrine system by inhibiting the secretion of adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH) and cortisol. Rarely, adrenocortical insufficiency has been reported in association with opioid use. Patients should seek immediate medical attention if they experience symptoms such as nausea, vomiting, loss of appetite, fatigue, weakness, dizziness, or hypotension. If adrenocortical insufficiency is suspected, confirm with diagnostic testing as soon as possible. If diagnosed, the patient should be treated with physiologic replacement doses of corticosteroids, and if appropriate, weaned off of opioid therapy. If the opioid can be discontinued, a follow-up assessment of adrenal function should be performed to determine if corticosteroid treatment can be discontinued. Other opioids may be tried; some cases reported use of a different opioid with no recurrence of adrenocortical insufficiency. It is unclear which, if any, opioids are more likely to cause adrenocortical insufficiency.

    Headline
    apnea, bleeding, bradycardia, cardiac arrest, chest pain (unspecified), edema, flushing, heart failure, hypertension, hypotension, orthostatic hypotension, palpitations, peripheral edema, peripheral vasodilation, respiratory arrest, sinus tachycardia, syncope, thrombosis
    General Information
    Oxycodone can cause cardiovascular adverse reactions due to anticholinergic effects and ability to release histamine. Cardiovascular effects reported in adult clinical trials of immediate-release and extended-release oxycodone tablets, respectively, include sinus bradycardia, sinus tachycardia (less than 3%, not reported), hypotension (less than 3%, not reported), heart failure (less than 3%, not reported), deep thrombophlebitis or thrombosis (less than 3%, not reported), bleeding or hemorrhage (less than 3%, not reported), and syncope (not reported, less than 1%); sinus tachycardia was also reported in 1% to 5% of patients during pediatric trials of the extended-release tablets. Orthostatic hypotension (1% to 5%) may arise as a secondary effect from peripheral vasodilation (less than 3%, not reported). Edema (less than 3%), facial edema (less than 1%), and peripheral edema (less than 3%) were noted in adult study patients. Palpitations (particularly in withdrawal) were reported in less than 3% of immediate-release oxycodone adult study patients and in postmarketing experience, while other cardiac symptoms such as chest pain (unspecified) and ST depression were reported in less than 1% of extended-release study patients. Edema, flushing, and hypertension were reported in 1% to 5% of patients treated with extended-release oxycodone capsules during clinical trials. Additionally, heart rate increases were noted in less than 1% of patients. In cases of overdose, severe respiratory and/or circulatory depression, respiratory arrest, apnea, shock, and cardiac arrest may occur. Oxycodone may cause severe hypotension including orthostatic hypotension and syncope in ambulatory patients as a result of peripheral vasodilation. Patients at increased risk for hypotensive effects include those whose ability to maintain blood pressure is already compromised by reduced blood volume or concurrent administration of certain CNS depressants (e.g., phenothiazines, general anesthetics).

    Headline
    blurred vision, miosis, mydriasis, urinary retention, xerostomia
    General Information
    Anticholinergic effects occur infrequently with oxycodone. Patients can experience xerostomia (less than 3% with immediate-release oxycodone, 6% with extended-release oxycodone tablets, not reported with extended-release oxycodone capsules), blurred vision or other abnormal vision (not reported with immediate-release, less than 1% with extended-release tablets, 1% to 5% with extended-release capsules), or urinary retention (not reported with immediate-release, less than 1% with extended-release tablets, not reported with extended-release capsules). Mydriasis can occur if, during an acute overdose for example, brain anoxia develops or drug withdrawal occurs. Therapeutic doses can increase accommodation and sensitivity to light reflex and decrease intraocular tension in both normal and glaucomatous eyes; the incidence of these effects during oxycodone therapy is not known.

    Headline
    hypertonia, myoclonia, tremor
    General Information
    As myoclonia has been reported in some patients receiving opioids, such effects may be seen in patients receiving oxycodone. Myoclonia incidence may increase with high doses of opioids; muscle twitching is reported more commonly among those on opioid therapy and at an incidence of 1% to 5% in adult study patients. Tremor and hypertonia have been reported in less than 3% of adult study patients receiving oxycodone immediate-release formulations, and tremor has been reported in 1% to 5% of patients receiving extended-release capsules. Clonazepam has been used to treat myoclonic jerks secondary to opioids; however, caution should be used in using benzodiazepines in combination with opioids due to additive CNS depression.

    asthenia, chills, dehydration, diaphoresis, fatigue, fever, hyperhidrosis, irritability, malaise, night sweats
    General Information
    Asthenia and diaphoresis (sweating) have been reported in 6% and 5% of adult patients receiving extended-release oxycodone, respectively; both symptoms were reported more commonly among oxycodone-treated as compared to placebo-treated patients. With immediate-release formulations, asthenia was reported in 3% or more of patients and diaphoresis in less than 3% of patients. Other general disorders reported with extended-release oxycodone in 1% to 5% of adult patients include chills, fever, fatigue, and irritability. Chills and fever were each reported in less than 3% of patients during trials of immediate-release oxycodone. Fever (11%), fatigue (1% to 5%), pain (1% to 5%), chills (1% to 5%), and asthenia (1% to 5%) were also reported in pediatric patients receiving extended-release oxycodone tablets during clinical safety trials. Thirst, dehydration, night sweats, and malaise have been reported in less than 1% of adult patients. Hyperhidrosis (1% to 5%) has been reported in pediatric patients receiving extended-release oxycodone tablets and adults receiving oxycodone extended-release capsules; it was reported in less than 3% of patients during trials of immediate-release oxycodone. Additionally, procedural pain and seroma were reported in 1% to 5% of pediatric patients receiving extended-release oxycodone tablets during clinical trials.

    Headline
    dysphagia, GI obstruction
    General Information
    GI obstruction and exacerbation of diverticulitis have been reported rarely during postmarketing use of OxyContin tablets. In some cases, surgical intervention was required to remove the tablet. The risk of these complications is greater in patients with underlying GI disorders including esophageal cancer or colon cancer with a small gastrointestinal lumen. OxyContin and Oxecta tablets may swell when exposed to liquids, including saliva. Difficulty swallowing resulting in choking, gagging, regurgitation, and tablets getting stuck in the throat has been reported during postmarketing experience with OxyContin. Use with caution in patients with difficulty swallowing or underlying GI disorders that may predispose them to obstruction. Instruct patients not to pre-soak, lick or otherwise wet OxyContin or Oxecta tablets prior to placing in the mouth, and to take 1 tablet at a time with enough water to ensure complete swallowing immediately after placing in the mouth. Dysphagia has occurred in less than 1% of adult clinical trial patients treated with extended-release oxycodone and in less than 3% with immediate-release.

    Headline
    cough, dyspnea, epistaxis, laryngospasm, pharyngitis, rhinitis, sinusitis
    General Information
    Adverse respiratory effects have been noted during oxycodone therapy. In addition to potentially serious respiratory depression associated with opioid use, the following respiratory adverse reactions have been reported in less than 3% of adult study patients: bronchitis, increased cough, epistaxis, laryngismus (laryngospasm), undefined lung disorder, pharyngitis, rhinitis, and sinusitis. Cough was observed in 1% to 5% of adults who received oxycodone extended-release capsules during clinical trials. Dyspnea has been reported in less than 3% of adult patients receiving immediate-release oxycodone, 1% to 5% receiving extended-release tablets, and less than 1% receiving extended-release capsules. Voice alterations were observed in less than 1% of adult patients during clinical trials of extended-release oxycodone tablets. Oropharyngeal pain (1% to 5%) has been reported during pediatric trials of the extended-release tablets and adult trials of the extended-release capsules.

    Headline
    serotonin syndrome
    General Information
    Serotonin syndrome has been reported in patients taking opioids at recommended doses. Patients taking opioids concomitantly with a serotonergic medication should seek immediate medical attention if they develop symptoms such as agitation, hallucinations, tachycardia, fever, excessive sweating, shivering or shaking, muscle twitching or stiffness, trouble with coordination, nausea, vomiting, or diarrhea. Symptoms generally present within hours to days of taking an opioid with another serotonergic agent, but may also occur later, particularly after a dosage increase. If serotonin syndrome is suspected, either the opioid and/or the other agent should be discontinued.
    Adverse Reaction GSTerms
      GSTerm Name
      serotonin syndrome
      Severity
      Severe
    Headline
    hyperalgesia
    General Information
    Cases of opioid-induced hyperalgesia (OIH) have been reported, both with short-term and longer-term use of opioids. OIH occurs when an opioid paradoxically causes an increase in pain or an increase in sensitivity to pain. Symptoms of OIH include increased levels of pain upon opioid dosage increase, decreased levels of pain upon opioid dosage decrease, or pain from ordinarily non-painful stimuli (allodynia). These symptoms may suggest OIH only if there is no evidence of underlying disease progression, opioid tolerance, opioid withdrawal, or addictive behavior. Data suggests a strong biologic plausibility between opioids and OIH and allodynia. If OIH is suspected, carefully consider appropriately decreasing the dose of the current opioid analgesic or opioid rotation (safely switching to a different opioid). Hyperalgesia has been reported with oxycodone extended-release tablets during postmarketing experience.
    Adverse Reaction GSTerms
      GSTerm Name
      hyperalgesia
      Severity
      Moderate

Description
  Text
  Oxycodone is an oral semisynthetic opioid agonist derived from the opioid alkaloid, thebaine, and is similar to other phenanthrene derivatives, such as hydrocodone and morphine. Oxycodone is approved for the treatment of moderate to severe pain. Oxycodone has been reported to be as effective as morphine in relieving pain when given in equianalgesic doses, but oxycodone causes less nausea, vomiting, and hallucinations. Oxycodone may have an improved toxicity profile in patients with impaired renal or hepatic function as compared to other opioid agonists.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  Mechanism of Action: Oxycodone is a potent µ-opiate receptor agonist. Opiate receptors include µ (mu), kappa (kappa), and delta (delta), which have been reclassified by an International Union of Pharmacology subcommittee as OP1 (delta), OP2 (kappa), and OP3 (µ). These receptors are coupled with G-protein (guanine-nucleotide-binding protein) receptors and function as modulators, both positive and negative, of synaptic transmission via G-proteins that activate effector proteins. Opioid-G-protein systems include adenylyl cyclase-cyclic adenosine monophosphate (cAMP) and phospholipase3 C (PLC)-inositol 1,4,5 triphosphate (Ins(1,4,5)P3)-Ca2). Opiates do not alter the pain threshold of afferent nerve endings to noxious stimuli, nor do they affect the conductance of impulses along peripheral nerves. Analgesia is mediated through changes in the perception of pain at the spinal cord (µ2- , delta,- kappa-receptors) and higher levels in the CNS (µ1- and kappa3 receptors). There is no ceiling effect of analgesia for opiates. The emotional response to pain is also altered. Opioids close N-type voltage-operated calcium channels (kappa-receptor agonist) and open calcium-dependent inwardly-rectifying potassium channels (µ and delta receptor agonist) resulting in hyperpolarization and reduced neuronal excitability. Binding of the opiate stimulates the exchange of guanosine triphosphate (GTP) for guanosine diphosphate (GDP) on the G-protein complex. Binding of GTP leads to a release of the G-protein subunit, which acts on the effector system. In this case of opioid-induced analgesia, the effector system is adenylate cyclase and cAMP located at the inner surface of the plasma membrane. Thus, opioids decrease intracellular cAMP by inhibiting adenylate cyclase that modulates the release of nociceptive neurotransmitters such as substance P, GABA, dopamine, acetylcholine and norepinephrine. Opioids also modulate the endocrine and immune systems. Opioids inhibit the release of vasopressin, somatostatin, insulin and glucagon.The stimulatory effects of opioids are the result of 'disinhibition' as the release of inhibitory neurotransmitters such as GABA and acetylcholine is blocked. The exact mechanism how opioid agonists cause both inhibitory and stimulatory processes is not well understood. Possible mechanisms including differential susceptibility of the opioid receptor to desensitization or activation of more than one G-protein system or subunit (one excitatory and one inhibitory) by an opioid receptor.Clinically, stimulation of µ-receptors produces analgesia, euphoria, respiratory depression, miosis, decreased gastrointestinal motility, and physical dependence. Kappa-receptor stimulation also produces analgesia, miosis, respiratory depression, as well as, dysphoria and some psychomimetic effects (i.e., disorientation and/or depersonalization). Miosis is produced by an excitatory action on the autonomic segment of the nucleus of the oculomotor nerve. Respiratory depression is caused by direct action of opiate agonists on respiratory centers in the brain stem. Opiate agonists increase smooth muscle tone in the antral portion of the stomach, the small intestine (especially the duodenum), the large intestine, and the sphincters. Opiate agonists also decrease secretions from the stomach, pancreas, and biliary tract. The combination of effects of opiate agonists on the GI tract results in constipation and delayed digestion. Urinary smooth muscle tone is also increased by opiate agonists. The tone of the bladder detrusor muscle, ureters, and vesical sphincter is increased, which sometimes causes urinary retention.Several other clinical effects occur with opiate agonists including cough suppression, hypotension, and nausea/vomiting. The antitussive effects of opiate agonists are mediated through direct action on receptors in the cough center of the medulla. Cough suppression can be achieved at lower doses than those required to produce analgesia. Hypotension is possibly due to an increase in histamine release and/or depression of the vasomotor center in the medulla. Induction of nausea and vomiting possibly occurs from direct stimulation of the vestibular system and/or the chemoreceptor trigger zone.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Oral Route
    Description
    Immediate-release preparations: Oral bioavailability is 60% to 87%, which is more than other opioid agonists due to low pre-systemic and first-pass metabolism. The Cmax occurs approximately 1 hour after administration of the oral solution and 1.3 hours after administration of tablets. Pharmacokinetics of RoxyBond tablets are similar to other immediate-release formulations. Oxecta brand is bioequivalent to other immediate-release tablet formulations in the fasted state. The onset of analgesia begins in about 15 minutes following dose administration, with maximal analgesic effect seen at 1 to 2 hours post-dose.
 
Extended-release tablets: Oral bioavailability is 60% to 87%. The relative oral bioavailability of the extended-release tablets to the immediate-release tablets is 100%. Release of oxycodone is pH independent. Peak plasma concentration and systemic exposure are dose-proportional for the 10 mg, 15 mg, 20 mg, 30 mg, 40 mg, 60 mg, and 80 mg tablet strengths. Food has no significant effect on the extent of absorption from the extended-release tablets. Steady-state plasma concentrations are reached within 24 to 36 hours after initiating dosing. Doses of 10 mg every 12 hours of the extended-release tablets were equivalent to the AUC and Cmax of 5 mg every 6 hours of the immediate-release tablets.
 
Extended-release capsules (Xtampza ER): Extended-release capsules are not bioequivalent to extended-release tablets. In the fed state, the AUC of the tablet and capsule is similar; however, the Cmax is lower for the capsule. In the fasted state, Cmax and AUC are both lower for the capsule. Time to peak concentration is approximately 3 hours longer for the capsule compared to immediate-release oral solution. The mean Cmax of oxycodone from the capsule compared to Cmax from oral solution is lower (73% and 43% lower for fasted and fed conditions, respectively). The relative bioavailability of the capsules to oxycodone immediate release oral solution is 75% in the fasted state and 114% in the fed state. The bioavailability of oxycodone from the capsule is greater when taken with food; bioavailability is dependent on the type of food consumed and fat content. Following a high-fat and high-calorie meal, increases in Cmax of 100% to 150% and AUC of 50% to 60% were seen. Following a medium-fat, medium calorie meal, Cmax increased by 84% and AUC by 28%. Following a low-fat, low-calorie meal, Cmax increased by 19% and AUC was comparable to the fasted state. Tmax occurs approximately 4.5 hours after administration in the fed state. Steady state concentrations are reached within 24 to 36 hours. Pharmacokinetic studies demonstrated no clinically relevant differences in Cmax, Tmax, and AUC when the capsule was given intact vs. sprinkled capsule contents under fed conditions.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Hepatic Impairment
    Description
    Patients with mildly to moderately impaired hepatic function have AUCs 95% and 65% higher for oxycodone and noroxycodone, respectively, and AUC 40% lower for oxymorphone than patients with normal hepatic function. In patients with mildly to moderately impaired hepatic function, the oxymorphone Cmax is 30% lower, the oxycodone Cmax is 50% higher, and the noroxycodone Cmax is 20% higher as compared with values from patients with normal hepatic function. Further, the elimination half-life of oxycodone is increased by 2.3 hours in patients with mildly to moderately impaired hepatic function. These differences are accompanied by increases in some, but not other, drug effects.
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    Patients with a CrCl less than 60 mL/minute have a decreased clearance of oxycodone, noroxycodone, and oxymorphone. Among 13 patients with mild to severe renal dysfunction (creatinine clearance less than 60 mL/minute), the systemic oxycodone exposure (AUC) was 60% higher, the noroxycodone AUC was 50% higher, and the oxymorphone AUC was 40% higher as compared with values from patients with normal renal function. Also, the peak plasma oxycodone concentration was 50% higher, and the peak noroxycodone concentration was 20% higher. An increase in the oxycodone elimination half-life of 1 hour was observed among the patients with renal impairment, and these patients had increased sedation but no differences in respiratory rate, pupillary constriction, or several other measures of drug effect as compared with effects seen in patients with normal renal function.
    Name
    Pediatrics
    Description
    The systemic exposure of extended-release oxycodone tablets (i.e., OxyContin) is expected to be similar in pediatric patients 11 years or older and adults at any given dose.
    Name
    Geriatric
    Description
    Plasma concentrations of oxycodone are only minimally affected by age and are 15% greater in the elderly as compared to young adults.
    Name
    Gender Differences
    Description
    Female patients, on average, have plasma oxycodone concentrations up to 20% to 25% higher than males on a weight-adjusted basis. The reason for this difference is unknown. The clinical relevance of this difference is low when giving chronic oxycodone at individualized doses.
  Text
  Oxycodone is administered orally. It is about 45% protein bound. Once absorbed, it is distributed to skeletal muscle, liver, intestinal tract, lungs, spleen, and the CNS; oxycodone has been found in breast milk. Metabolism occurs in the liver with excretion principally in the urine. Oxycodone is metabolized to noroxycodone by CYP3A4, noroxymorphone by both CYP2D6 and CYP3A4, and oxymorphone by CYP2D6. The major circulating metabolite is noroxycodone with an AUC ratio of 0.6 relative to that of oxycodone. Noroxycodone is reported to be a considerably weaker analgesic than oxycodone. The analgesic effect is primarily due to the parent compound; oxymorphone has analgesic effects, but it is only present in low concentrations. The elimination half-life of oxycodone is 3 to 5 hours with the duration of analgesia lasting 3 to 4 hours. Extended-release tablets have a slightly longer half-life (3.8 to 4.5 hours) than the immediate-release preparations (3.2 hours). The half-life with the extended-release capsule is 5.6 hours when dosed in the fed state.
 
Affected cytochrome P450 isoenzymes and drug transporters: CYP3A4, CYP2D6
Oxycodone is a CYP3A4 and CYP2D6 substrate.

Administration
  General Information
  For storage information, see the specific product information within the How Supplied section.
  Route Specific Administrations
    Administration Details
      Name
      Oral Solid Formulations
      Description
      Immediate-release tablets:
May be administered with food or milk to minimize GI irritation.
Oxecta and Oxaydo brand tablets: Swallow whole; do not crush or dissolve. Do not pre-soak, lick, or otherwise wet tablet prior to dose administration. Administer 1 tablet at a time; allow patient to swallow each tablet separately with sufficient liquid to ensure prompt and complete transit through the esophagus. Do not use this brand for administration via nasogastric, gastric, or other feeding tubes as it may cause obstruction of feeding tubes.
 
Extended-release tablets (e.g., OxyContin):
Administer whole; do not crush, chew, cut, dissolve, or break in half. Taking chewed, broken, cut, dissolved, or crushed extended-release tablets could lead to the rapid release and absorption of a potentially fatal dose of oxycodone.
May be administered with or without food.
Take with a full glass of water to ensure complete swallowing.
In general, administer one-half of the patient's total daily dose every 12 hours. If asymmetric dosing is necessary, instruct patient to take the higher dose in the morning.
Extended-release 60 mg and 80 mg tablets are for use ONLY in opioid-tolerant patients.
Monitor patients closely for respiratory depression, particularly within the first 24 to 72 hours after initiation or dose escalation.
OxyContin: Do not pre-soak, lick, or otherwise wet tablet prior to dose administration. Administer 1 tablet at a time; allow patient to swallow each tablet separately with sufficient liquid to ensure prompt and complete transit through the esophagus.
Roxybond: The biologically inert components of this tablet may remain intact and appear as a tablet in the stool.
 
Extended-release capsules (Xtampza ER):
Always take with food and with approximately the same amount of food in order to ensure consistent plasma concentrations.
The capsule contents may be taken by sprinkling the contents onto soft foods (e.g., applesauce, pudding, yogurt, ice cream, or jam) or into a cup and then giving directly into the mouth. Swallow immediately and rinse mouth to ensure all capsule contents have been swallowed. Discard capsule shells following administration.
The capsule contents may be given through a nasogastric or gastrostomy tube. Flush the tube with water. Open a capsule and pour the contents directly into the tube. Do not pre-mix capsule contents with the liquid that will be used to flush the tube. Draw up 15 mL of water into a syringe, insert the syringe into the tube, and flush the contents through the tube. Repeat flushing twice using 10 mL of water with each flush. Milk or liquid nutritional supplement may be used as an alternative to water when flushing capsule contents through the tube.
Extended-release 36 mg capsules are for use ONLY in opioid-tolerant patients.
Monitor patients closely for respiratory depression, particularly within the first 24 to 72 hours after initiation or dose escalation.
      Name
      Oral Liquid Formulations
      Description
      Concentrated (20 mg/mL) solution:
Always use the included oral syringe when administering the highly concentrated solution; take care in dispensing and administering this medication.
For ease of administration, the concentrated solution may be added to 30 mL of a liquid or semi-solid food. If the medication is placed in liquid or food, the patient needs to immediately consume; do not store diluted oxycodone for future use.
    Name
    Oral Administration
    Description
    Oxycodone should be titrated from the initial recommended dosage to the dose required to relieve the patient's pain and minimize adverse reactions.
There is no maximum dose of oxycodone; however, careful titration is required to avoid adverse reactions (i.e., drowsiness and respiratory depression).
Storage: Keep oxycodone secured in a location not accessible by others.
Disposal: Flush unused oxycodone down the toilet when it is no longer needed if a drug take-back option is not readily available.

  Age Groups
    Name
    Adults

    Immediate-release dosage forms, extended-release tablets: There is no maximum dose of oxycodone; however, careful titration of oxycodone, especially in opiate-naive patients, is required until tolerance develops to some of the side effects (i.e., drowsiness and respiratory depression). Individualize dosage carefully.
Extended-release capsules (Xtampza ER): 288 mg/day PO (equivalent to 320 mg/day oxycodone hydrochloride).
    Name

Individualize dosage carefully.

    Name
    Adolescents

Immediate-release dosage forms and extended-release capsules (Xtampza ER): Safety and efficacy have not been established.
    Name
    Children

    Extended-release tablets in Children 11 years or older: With appropriate dosage titration, there is no maximum dose of extended-release oxycodone in opioid-tolerant pediatric patients; however, careful titration is required until tolerance develops to some of the side effects (i.e., drowsiness and respiratory depression). Individualize dosage carefully.
Extended-release tablets in Children younger than 11 years, immediate-release dosage forms, and extended-release capsules (Xtampza ER): Safety and efficacy have not been established.
    Name
    Infants
    Max Dosage
    Safety and efficacy have not been established.
    Name
    Neonates
    Max Dosage
    Safety and efficacy have not been established.

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  Start initial therapy at one-third to one-half the normal dose and titrate dose carefully. Patients with hepatic impairment have higher plasma oxycodone and noroxycodone and lower oxymorphone concentrations than those with normal hepatic function.
  Renal Impairment
  Conservative initial dose and dose titration are required. Dosage should be modified depending on clinical response and degree of renal impairment. In patients with CrCl less than 60 mL/minute, the serum concentration of oxycodone is about 50% higher than in patients with normal renal function.
